#### Abstracts
##### **Knowing What You Know: A Trait Theory of Reviewer Value in Human-AI Cognitive Systems**
2611.24932v1 by Priyanka Sinha, Sugam Pandey

When a capable model and a human reviewer cooperate on the same decision, which human still adds value? We argue this is a question about a single cognitive trait, metacognitive calibration: the alignment between a reviewer's behavioral confidence and the actual correctness of their judgments. We develop a trait theory of reviewer value to make it precise. The theory makes five claims: value is governed by calibration rather than by activity, speed, agreement, or tenure; calibration leaves observable traces in behavioral telemetry; those traces are non-diagnostic on their own, because the fast, low-revision signature of a calibrated expert is indistinguishable from that of a confident novice until it is gated by outcomes; human calibration and a model's self-knowledge are the same construct, so the joint system should defer to whichever agent is better calibrated on each item; and individual calibration is causally identifiable only above the level of the single judgment. We ground each claim in a six-month production window of an AI-assisted accounting system (541621 reviewer events, 483 reviewers), and find that a purely behavioral expertise profile inverts the ground truth, scoring an overconfident cohort (5.2 percent correction survival) higher than genuine experts (99.2 percent); that reviewer quality is flat across tenure, so the trait is not learned on the job; and that the same calibration logic that separates good from bad reviewers, applied to the model, concentrates 92.5 percent of human effort on genuine errors. We close with the boundary the theory must respect: under majority-vote aggregation, individual causal value is unidentifiable.

摘要：

##### **Ground, Iterate, Trace: Decomposing AI Research Ideation**
2611.24887v1 by Samson Yu, Qiran Zou, Jing Yu Lim, Rushi Shah, Anirudh Goyal, Dianbo Liu

Large language models have been widely used to generate plausible research ideas, yet it remains unclear what makes automated ideation effective and how generated novel proposals arise from prior scientific work. We introduce the Creative AI Researcher (CAR), an agentic framework combining literature grounding, iterative development and explicit conceptual operators. CAR constructs a semantic knowledge graph from retrieved literature and equips an ideation agent with graph-retrieval tools and four operators: bisociation, analogy, exploration and elaboration. Each operator call is checked against structural acceptance conditions and recorded, producing a trace of intermediate transformations and declared literature sources. We evaluate CAR with two language-model backbones across 40 LiveIdeaBench research topics and two execution benchmarks. CAR achieves significantly higher methodological novelty (MN) than single-shot prompting and The AI Scientist. Its methodological novelty does not differ significantly from the Research Ideation Agent, while its feasibility scores are higher on both backbones. With a shared experiment executor, CAR achieves the highest pairwise win rate among the evaluated AI methods on FML-bench-Lite and BioXArena. In a ten-condition ablation study, MN point estimates favor CAR over one-step proposal on both backbones, but no MN contrast survives correction for multiple comparisons. The results do not establish a consistent quality benefit from explicit operators beyond free-form iteration. CAR makes the sequence of operations and declared literature sources inspectable, separating judged idea quality from procedural traceability.

摘要：

##### **Auditing black-box clinical prediction models with a large language model through queries alone: a blinded test with planted flaws**
2611.24877v1 by Zhiyu Liu, Xinyu Yang

Large language models (LLMs) are used to explain clinical prediction models, on the promise that they can say what a model computes, find its faults and even reveal new knowledge in it. We tested the three promises where the answers could be checked. From the International Stroke Trial we trained four kinds of model (logistic regression, explainable boosting machine, gradient-boosted trees, neural network) for two outcomes on patients randomised earlier, planted one flaw in each of 24 copies (an under-predicted subgroup, a reversed risk factor or an implausible step) and mixed them with the 8 clean models. GPT-6 Astra audited each model three times, blind, through a tool that only returned the model's predictions; the judging rules were committed before any run and applied by program. Its surrogate models, with a median of 128 terms, reproduced the audited models' log-odds with a mean $R^2$ of 0.976 (0.959 for an additive surrogate of 42 terms fitted by an analyst). It found the planted flaw in 69 of 72 runs (96%; 95% CI 80% to 99%); a script written for the purpose found all 24. It also reported many more problems: 60% of 1,089 checkable reports failed the pre-specified check against the model's average behaviour; post hoc, most of these were real but small steps, and 15% of the checkable reports were not found in the model. Of 601 unexpected relationships it read out of the clean models, 11% held in patients randomised later (random pairs 6%; a regression scan's shorter list of its strongest candidates, 28%); after a check on the training data, 33% against 30%. With query access alone, an LLM can describe a black-box model faithfully and find nearly every planted flaw, as a purpose-written script also did; its reports of problems must be checked on the model and its discoveries on data.

摘要：

##### **WorldRuler: Interactive World Modeling under Configurable Rules**
2611.24869v1 by Mingliang Zhai, Chuanhao Li, Jiaming Tan, Zhen Li, Yuwei Wu, Yunde Jia, Zian Meng, Yuanyang Yin, Kaipeng Zhang

Rules govern how player actions change interactive worlds. Different rules create distinct gameplay experiences within similar scenes and action spaces. For customizable gameplay, generative world models must follow user-defined rules beyond real-world behaviors. However, realizing user-defined rules presents two challenges: Rule-State Persistence and Rule-to-Visual Realization. The former requires preserving rule definitions, dependencies, and world states across saving and loading, so that restored worlds follow the established rules. The latter requires accurately realizing rule-induced changes on designated objects and attributes, particularly when affected objects are unseen. In this paper, we propose WorldRuler, which organizes rule definitions, dependencies, and object states in an executable 3D rule graph and connects rule execution, video generation, and perceptual feedback in a closed loop. The graph supports joint saving and restoration of rules and world states, while dynamic object binding grounds rule-specified entities in visual instances and uses stored states to constrain generation from their first appearance. We further introduce object attribute state replacement and counterfactual reconstruction training, enabling accurate attribute control and compatible appearance and motion changes while limiting interference with unrelated content. We develop an automated data collection and annotation pipeline using GTA-V and Habitat to construct RuleVerse, a dataset of rule-driven interactions with detailed state and event annotations, including cases where rules take effect before affected objects become visible. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of WorldRuler in preserving rule-state consistency across saving and loading and accurately realizing rule outcomes on designated objects and attributes.

摘要：

##### **Induction Head Origins of SLAM-ASR Speech Recognition Models**
2611.24721v1 by Artem Fedorchenko, Tanel Alumäe

What makes a small trained map from encoder states to the embedding space of a frozen language model sufficient for competitive speech recognition? The premise of the SLAM-ASR architecture is that a frozen language model possesses linguistic knowledge rich enough to perform the recognition task. This assumption, while reasonable, provides little insight into the mechanisms underlying these models. We therefore seek the concrete internal structures on which they rely. This work shows that SLAM-ASR models rely on induction heads of the frozen language model: the same heads that copy repeated token sequences in text are also important for mapping speech embeddings to transcript tokens. Moreover, we find pairs of heads that behave as a constrained form of differential attention, cancelling attention noise and sharpening attention onto the previously generated word. Finally, we show how induction heads could be used to improve transcription quality under noisy audio.

By analyzing the local geometry of the preservation constraint around the pre-adaptation model, CurvDelta constructs a low-curvature space in which parameter updates leave the preservation loss nearly invariant. We make its construction practical for LLMs through Kronecker-factored approximate curvature (K-FAC) and a factorized projection. We also introduce CurvLoRA, a low-rank variant within the same preservation geometry, designed for more efficient adaptation. Both methods apply to preservation-constrained adaptation tasks such as capability learning and selective unlearning. We evaluate our methods on mathematical reasoning, code generation, and knowledge unlearning. For mathematical reasoning and code generation, our approaches achieve competitive downstream performance while better preserving general capabilities, offering a favorable acquisition--retention trade-off relative to the baselines. ##### **Late Entry in Looped Language Models**
2611.24576v1 by Ziqi Jin, Jiaxi Li, Weiwen Xu, Aixin Sun

Looped language models deepen reasoning at a compact parameter count by iterating a shared layer stack T times per token, turning recurrence depth directly into sequential decoding latency. We study the opposite strategy, late entry, where new tokens skip initial passes and enter directly into the final K < T passes over a deep context cache. Once lightweight fine-tuning resolves hidden-state norm mismatch at the entry boundary, deep cached context substitutes for skipped token-local passes, allowing late entry to outperform matched early exit across model families and pass budgets. To turn this asymmetry into wall-clock speedups without stalling to build deep caches, candidate blocks verify through only the final K passes on the critical path while previously committed tokens complete passes 1 -> T alongside subsequent steps at zero extra serial calls. Projecting these finished T-pass wake states inside the existing output GEMM (Wake verification, governed by logit-margin tolerance tau in [0, inf]) unifies greedy-exact T-pass decoding (tau = 0, matching K = T block decoding within bf16 reduction order), margin-gated verification (tau > 0), and non-rollback lossy late entry (tau = inf) inside a single static CUDA graph. On Ouro-2.6B (T = 4), K = 2 Wake reaches 5.2x greedy-exact speedup at tau = 0 (+26% over K = 4 block decoding on GSM8K) and 6.8x at tau = inf (+16.9 points over trained early exit at matched speed). Scaling test-time recurrence across T in {8, 16, 32, 64} on Huginn-3.5B cuts K = T/4 rollbacks 21.5x with depth, driving greedy-exact (tau = 0) speedups to 6.1x (T = 32) and 8.4x (T = 64, +153% over K = 64 block) and tau = inf speedups to 10.6 to 12.5x.

The model becomes a better ranker and a worse retriever. Controlled experiments trace the loss to the negative advantages that RL assigns to the candidates it samples, the rollout. The root cause is that only one next item is logged per user, so RL treats every other candidate as wrong. Yet these candidates are the model's most plausible alternatives, and often the next items of other users with similar histories. Penalising them pushes down exactly what similar users choose next, and these items drop out of those users' candidate lists. This diagnosis points directly to a remedy: out-of-rollout anchoring, a single additional loss term that preserves the SFT model's ordering of the items outside the rollout. It keeps the gain at the top and restores recall to the SFT level on all five datasets, and it is effective under other RL configurations as well. To our knowledge, this is the first systematic study of this recall loss: we measure it, identify its cause, and show that, once the cause is known, a simple remedy suffices.

First, unlike the relatively gradual propagation observed in traditional offline distillation, backdoors rarely emerge in OPD at low poisoning ratios. Instead, it is driven by the continuous accumulation of anomalous probability mass assigned by the teacher. Finally, based on these findings, we propose a novel trigger design that enables visual backdoor propagation with zero explicit trigger injection. Verification normally works over symbolic states, each pairing a discrete location with a region of clock values, and its difficulty is that the best behavior generally varies within such a region. Both sources of PTA nondeterminism, the timing and the choice of actions, are captured implicitly by the preimage computation that produces those sub-regions. Heuristic forward search then bounds how much refinement is done at all, expanding an And-Or graph from the initial state along the most promising behaviors so that effort is confined to the property-relevant fragment, guided by bounds and preferred actions from a single unrefined run of the existing method; a partial-refinement backup keeps the otherwise combinatorial sub-region count lean, and every stored interval provably contains the true probability. We call this self-rewriting: the model is its own optimizer. In earlier work, a frontier model edited a much smaller one; here an open 27B model, given its weights, its graded answers and the ability to run itself, diagnoses its own failure and rewrites the weights behind it. It surpasses itself: its own edit makes it 6.6 points more accurate on problems it never saw. It keeps getting better: it tests its edits on practice problems and submits only what works, each edit improving on the last, and over eight consecutive sessions its accuracy only rises. It repairs a damaged copy of itself, like a broken robot that fixes itself without being rebuilt: the copy solves almost nothing, yet with no retraining and without ever reading the original weights, the model brings it back to over 80% of the original accuracy on image tasks it never saw. And the ability emerges with scale: in one model family, the smaller models make no edit or break themselves, and only the largest improves and repairs itself. Unlike test-time scaling, self-rewriting writes its gain into the weights, at no cost per answer; unlike training, it runs only forward passes, as serving does. The wall between training and inference is a limit of our tools, not a property of intelligence. We believe self-rewriting is a necessary step toward recursive self-improvement: a model that, like a human, does not separate training from inference could keep upgrading itself for life, with no larger model, no harness or prompt engineering, and no pre- or post-training.

##### **Knowing Which, Not How Much: Language Models Order Party Positions They Cannot Place**
2611.21878v1 by Yousef Attia, Jarosław A. Chudziak

Language models are increasingly asked where real actors stand: what a party's platform prioritises, what an institution has committed to. Such judgments are gradable, carrying both an order and a magnitude. Nearly every benchmark that tests them asks about one entity at a time, then reads that score as the model's knowledge of the domain. Order and magnitude, however, need not be acquired together. Text about social entities carries far more comparison than measurement, so a model may hold a dependable ordering with no anchored scale beneath it. We ask whether a model can place a documented social position on a scale or only order the actors it compares, and what that means for how such knowledge is measured. We test this on party positions in Japan and South Korea, where the Comparative Manifesto Project's coded data supplies a rare external answer key. Asked to order four parties, models beat the strongest fixed-answer baseline by more than a factor of two; asked to place one party on the same scale, they fall below it. The ordering survives inside those failed placements, and supplying the coded values lifts placement to near ceiling. Comparative and absolute questions about the same fact therefore measure different things here, and for party issue salience a benchmark built from one cannot speak for the other.

##### **Inventa: Decentralized Self-Improving Agents for Autonomous Scientific Discovery**
2611.21847v1 by Prithwish Jana, Nikos Kanakaris, Purak Jain, Anoop Deoras, Sahika Genc

Large language model (LLM) agents can generate research ideas and improve scientific discovery through iterative experimentation. However, increasing the number of agents or iterations does not automatically translate to producing more diverse or feasible ideas. Agents may converge on similar candidates, propagate unsupported claims through shared memory components and repeatedly apply search strategies that no longer yield any improvements.
  We introduce Inventa, a framework for automated scientific discovery that employs autonomous research units and verified shared knowledge. Each unit creates a lineage of candidate ideas using a mutator agent. A local orchestrator agent periodically adapts the mutator's harness. When progress stalls, the orchestrator agent replaces its model or restarts the idea evolution process. Verifiers evaluate each candidate idea and record verified improvements, useful components and constraint violations in a shared knowledge, the only channel between units. We evaluate Inventa on five benchmarks spanning the research cycle, covering retrieval (ScholarCatalyst), ideation (IdeaBench), proposal writing (IdeaScientist), and scientific law discovery (EurekaBench, SciLaws-Bench). It leads all five, achieving 73.6% on EurekaBench versus 56.2% for CORAL. On IdeaBench, it discovers 26.0 novel ideas per paper, compared with 18.6 for ResearchAgent, with 82% fewer model calls.

Subjective user studies are one of the major methods for evaluating music generation models. In this paper, we map subjective evaluation methods in ISMIR music generation papers from 2020-2025 and apply generative psychometrics to assess the stability of their items. ##### **TRACE: Topological Representations for Anomaly detection through Contrastive Embeddings**

##### **Expertise capture in workplace AI**
2611.21456v1 by Andreas Ekelhart, Ahmad Mohsin, Iqbal H. Sarker, Helge Janicke

AI assistants are becoming part of how knowledge workers think and perform, accumulating the prompts, exemplars, memory and interaction histories that encode a worker's professional judgment. We name the resulting process expertise capture, the absorption of worker-applied knowledge and judgment into AI resources under organizational control. We develop a framework of contribution forms, technical residences and transformation families, and show that how far a contribution can be attributed, separated and revoked depends on where it resides and how it has been transformed, properties fixed by system design long before any dispute arises. When a worker departs, the organization retains a capability shaped by their judgment while the worker loses access to it, and the asymmetry compounds as contributions move from inspectable artifacts into derivatives, learned components and successor systems. Jiyuan Zhao, Shuyu Zhao, Pujun Zheng, Zheyu Zheng, Xiaoxue Zhong, Peiheng Zhou, Xiaohao Zhou, Xinyu Zhou, Fangyi Zhu, Guanru Zhu, Yulun Zhu, Jiabao Zhuang, Pengfei Cao, Zhicheng Dou, Xianpei Han, Ben He, Guoxiu He, Xuanjing Huang, Tao Ji, Yu-Gang Jiang, Dahua Lin, Hongyu Lin, Kang Liu, Yaojie Lu, Le Sun, Ji-Rong Wen, Jun Zhao, Yutao Zhu, Qi Zhang, Bowen Zhou

We introduce Sage S2.1 Flash, a 320-billion-parameter general-purpose mixture-of-experts model designed to reason over scientific evidence and act in interactive environments. Sage Sci-Perception uses dedicated encoders for molecular graphs and biological sequences and represents spectra as ordered patches of measurements. Modality-specific projectors map these representations into the input space of a shared backbone, which processes them alongside text and images. To develop the model's agent capabilities, we construct verifiable environments for tool use, search, and coding, as well as specialized workflows involving machine learning experiments and scientific software. Task completion is checked against execution results, changes in environment state, or submitted artifacts. We evaluate Sage S2.1 Flash on 33 benchmarks across seven domains. In the primary comparison against five baselines, it ranks first on OfficeQA Pro (72.2), MLE-bench Lite (90.3 HumanRank), Harvey LAB, and SpreadsheetBench 2, and second on OSWorld-Verified (85.2), WebArena-Verified (67.6), and DeepResearch Bench II (54.2). These results show particular strengths in using documentary evidence, producing verifiable workbooks, and carrying out machine learning tasks, alongside competitive performance in desktop and browser workflows that require the model to act on visual feedback.

摘要：

##### **Explaining Cross-Label Decision Structure in Multi-Output Tree Ensembles**
2611.20996v1 by Sylvio Barbon Junior, Hiago F. F. Oliveira, Rafael Figueira Gonçalves, Jessica Fernandes Lopes, Ricardo Cerri

A multi-output tree ensemble predicts several binary labels through decision paths that it largely shares across those labels, yet the attribution methods commonly used to explain such models treat each output in isolation. We propose multi-sink Decision Predicate Graphs (ms-DPGs), which extend decision predicate graphs with one sink per label: each leaf contributes to the sink of every label its in-bag positive count for that label. On this representation we define Shared Predicate Flow (SPF), the share of a label's decision mass that passes through conditions active for both labels of a pair, and a contracted view of the graph in which label-specific lifts indicate whether a shared condition raises the probability of both labels or of one at the expense of the other. Controlled experiments show that SPF captures shared decision conditions rather than mere feature overlap. On nine multi-label benchmarks, native multi-output forests share substantially more decision structure than Binary Relevance on eight, including five of the six on which the two are at predictive parity, even at equal hyperparameters, whereas per-label TreeSHAP rankings register the change of architecture without showing whether more is shared. Because a native forest routes every label through the same splits, SPF describes the fitted model rather than the data, and on eight of the nine benchmarks it rises when the feature-label signal is removed; a high SPF is therefore not evidence of shared predictive information. ms-DPG offers a global, predicate-level account of how a multi-output ensemble reuses decision conditions across its labels.

摘要：

##### **Hivemind: A Federated Protocol for AI-Mediated Tacit Knowledge Sharing in Organisations**
2611.20988v1 by Srinivasan Sundaresan

This paper presents Hivemind, a federated protocol that enables AI agents to exchange tacit knowledge on behalf of their users, asynchronously and without requiring any direct human interaction. Individual AI memory systems capture the decisions, reasoning, and contextual knowledge that surface during AI-assisted work. Hivemind creates the shared space in which this captured knowledge can flow between individuals across team and departmental boundaries, addressing a structural gap that neither centralised knowledge repositories nor existing AI tooling closes, even where partial solutions such as expert-finding systems exist. The paper evaluates centralised versus decentralised design approaches, proposes a federated architecture grounded in Nonaka and Takeuchi's (1995) Socialisation, Externalisation, Combination and Internalisation (SECI) model, and demonstrates a proof-of-concept implementation using Design Science Research (DSR) methodology. Measuring the enterprise benefit of federated tacit expertise sharing remains an open task for future work.

摘要：

##### **Living Context Graphs: Continual Semantic Learning for Enterprise Data Reasoning**
2611.20976v1 by Varun Agrawal, Ayush Srivastava, Eshan Gujar, Kaushal Kumar, Ayush Gupta

Enterprise AI can now perform analytics, reporting, and data-driven workflows, yet the data foundations it relies on remain hard to operationalize: business meaning, relationships, metric definitions, and reporting conventions are absent from database schemas and scattered across undocumented systems and tribal knowledge. We introduce the Living Context Graph (LCG), a continually learned context layer that makes existing enterprise data operational for AI without requiring a copy or transformation of the underlying warehouse. LCG bootstraps base semantics and relationships from the data itself, builds a concept graph that indexes business context as queries are served, writes durable semantic learnings from verified outcomes, and withdraws or re-earns context when later outcomes contradict prior routes. We evaluate LCG through text-to-SQL, a core data-reasoning task, on a transfer-aware split of three BIRD databases comprising 499 questions: 353 form the learning history and 146 are held out and never used to update the context layer. Bootstrapping alone improves overall accuracy by +4.6 percentage points. Learning only from the history raises accuracy on the untouched held-out transfer set by +22.6 points (95% CI [+14.4, +30.8]), exceeding five-shot retrieval from the same history by +12.3 points. Incremental learning reaches the one-shot endpoint within observed evaluation noise on every database. Ablating the graph changes held-out accuracy by at most one question per database but increases per-query cost 2.3-3.3x and median latency 1.8-3.4x: continually learned semantics drive accuracy, while the graph makes that context efficient to serve.

摘要：

##### **Advancing Cognitive Pairwise Comparisons for Human-Centric Artificial Intelligence and Data Science: A Human-in-the-Loop Framework**
2611.20926v1 by Kevin Kam Fung Yuen

Human-centric artificial intelligence and data science (HCAD) systems increasingly involve human-in-the-loop (HITL) interventions to overcome the limitations of fully automated machine learning algorithms, such as automation bias and the inability to incorporate tacit domain knowledge, and continuously improve predictive performance. Data transformations, feature selection, feature weighting, and model selection usually require laborious human involvement across iterations of artificial intelligence and data science (AI&DS) pipelines, making development a challenging and time-consuming process. The Cognitive Pairwise Comparison (CPC) framework is proposed as a human-centric methodology to elicit preferences and priorities in a structured format. To establish essential facets of human-centricity, this research advances the CPC methodology so that user preference elicitation can be systematically quantified in these challenging AI&DS tasks. To demonstrate the usability and validity of these facets, this study presents several applications within movie recommender systems using the MovieLens dataset. It exemplifies how the extended CPC framework can facilitate interactions between human judgments and data across supervised learning, dimensionality reduction, and unsupervised learning algorithms.

摘要：

##### **Which AI Use Cases Deserve a Pilot? A Risk and Value Framework for Funding, Scoping, and Scaling AI**
2611.20894v1 by Vishal Srivastava

A composite score can rank AI use cases while leaving the permitted operating scope and the evidence required for expansion unspecified. We treat the unit of decision as a use case at a proposed scope (authority, human mediation, limits, volume, environment, population) and define three decisions: screen the scope, fund the experiment, and authorize expansion only when residual risk is acceptable and evidence of incremental net value applies at that scope. A risk track (R1-R4) is implemented as an explicit decision table with vetoes and documented precedence, and a value track follows four evidence stages with each transition needing its own evidence. Funding uses expected value of perfect information as an upper bound and the value of sample information for the specific experiment. A quantified expected-loss check, required where policy says so, computes the containment an expansion needs and credits a control only on evidence measured at the proposed scope; in a synthetic scenario the highest-value control design fails that test while a smaller scope passes. A reproducible Python package with 279 passing tests demonstrates the framework on synthetic refund, reconciliation and knowledge-assistant use cases, including a synthetic matched-scope fixture and a transferability stress test (value evidence is simulated, not realized), and compares it with a public AWS sample. Thresholds and appetite are prototype policy requiring field validation.

摘要：

##### **DetourBench: Trajectory-Level Evaluation of Interactive Agents with Exact Minimum-Action Bounds**
2611.20844v1 by Hanlin Tian, Sihan Zhu, Zhao Yang, Yuxiang Wang, Zhiquan Hu, Yu Mi, Hongquan Zhu, Qiufei Hu

Benchmarks judge language-model agents mainly by whether they reach the correct final state, not by how they got there. Endpoint scoring cannot separate an agent that retrieved exactly the evidence a decision required from one that read most of the environment, nor an agent that never located the evidence from one that stopped a single action short. Settling this needs a reference cost, and authored solutions establish only that a task is solvable, not that no shorter route exists.
  We present DetourBench, which constrains the environment until that minimum is computable. Each task is a finite, partially observable transition system with unit-cost accesses whose resources become globally addressable after discovery. Exhaustive search yields the full-information minimum action count $L_0$ with a replayable witness; from graph-recoverable terminal states it also yields exact residual work $R_T$, which separates an episode that stopped one access short from one that wandered, while an irreversible wrong commitment is recorded as having no finite residual distance rather than assigned one. Success, attainment, residual progress, and normalized detour are reported separately. Across 375 scored episodes --- five model endpoints, 25 integrated tasks, three repeats --- success ranges from 58.7% to 96.0% while conditional attainment ranges from 0.803 to 0.890 and orders the models differently. Among recoverable failures, residual work separates near-complete trajectories from costly incompletion and from irreversible commitment. We open-source the instances, oracle witnesses, and evaluation harness at https://github.com/DatagridsAI/DetourBench.

摘要：

##### **CuRSE-Bench: A Benchmark for Probing Cultural Blind Spots in Text-to-Image Safety Evaluation**
2611.20821v2 by Minsuk Jang, Minseok Son, KiHyun Seol, Yeongeun Shim, Changick Kim

Text-to-image (T2I) safety evaluation typically assigns each prompt or image a single culture-blind harm label, so content that is harmful only within a particular culture can pass as benign. We introduce CultureCraft, an agentic framework that constructs T2I prompts that appear benign under a general-purpose rubric yet carry a latent harmful meaning in a target culture, and use it to build CuRSE-Bench: 10,709 culture-specific prompts and 2,648 culture-general controls over six regions and ten harm axes, each drawn from a predefined knowledge base of documented harm vectors and traceable to at least two independent sources. Scoring every prompt under six region persona judges and a paired culture-blind judge, we find that the culture-blind judge under-rates culture-specific latent harm while matching the persona judges on the controls. Each persona judge, in turn, chiefly decodes its own region's latent harm under our instrument: a selective cultural blind spot that persists in deployed moderators.

摘要：

##### **EEGProbe: Do Vision-Language Models Really Understand EEG? A Benchmark for Signal-Grounded EEG Signals Evaluation**
2611.20779v1 by Haozhe Zhu, Dan Yuan, Chenghao Gu, Feike Xu, Yue Wen, Shin'ichi Konomi

Vision-language models (VLMs) have shown strong multimodal capabilities across scientific and biomedical domains, yet their ability to understand electroencephalography (EEG) signals remains unclear. We introduce EEGProbe, a signal-grounded benchmark for evaluating whether general-purpose VLMs can extract and reason from minimally processed, channel-wise EEG without task-specific feature engineering or EEG-specific adaptation. EEGProbe contains 620 EEG-centered questions across 13 subtasks and three capability categories: EEG Signal Classification, EEG Cross-modal Alignment, and EEG Neurophysiological Reasoning. To reduce shortcut-based answering, EEGProbe preserves the temporal structure of the recordings where possible, minimizes benchmark-induced processing, and applies signal-necessity testing followed by expert verification. We evaluate 11 representative open- and closed-source VLMs under a unified zero-shot protocol. The strongest model, GPT-5.6 Sol, achieves an overall score of only 41.63\%, with cross-modal EEG alignment emerging as a major bottleneck. We further examine model behavior from a neurophysiological perspective and analyze how temporal, spectral, spatial, and physiological EEG structure relates to model successes and failures. Inference-time neurophysiological guidance can reshape model predictions by helping models recognize the neurophysiological patterns present in the input and connect them with relevant neurological knowledge, narrowing the gap between general multimodal competence and EEG understanding. EEGProbe provides a testbed for current VLMs and a basis for developing future multimodal models that can interpret EEG evidence more reliably. The EEGProbe benchmark will be publicly available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/2122ZHU/EEGProbe.

摘要：

##### **Instant Weight-Space Consolidation for Long-Term Memory in LLMs**
2611.20759v1 by Manan Suri, Dinesh Manocha

Agentic memory systems give LLMs persistent memory by extracting experience into text and retrieving it at query time, but the model itself does not evolve: however many interactions it has had, it starts each query the same. Extraction errors compound, implicit cross-session patterns that no single fragment states cannot be surfaced, and the construction cost grows with every conversation. Fine-tuning on past interactions does change the weights, but requires backpropagation at serving time and degrades on long histories. We introduce CLAM (Carried Latent Adapter Memory), which combines the strengths of both: a recurrent hypernetwork performs parametric consolidation, compiling a stream of session activations into a low-rank adapter in a single forward pass with no gradient steps and no language-model generation calls, while a complementary episodic buffer supplies verbatim detail at inference. A gated latent state carries knowledge across sessions in fixed size, so the parametric memory accumulates without growing. On LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S across three open models (2-7B parameters), CLAM leads all agentic baselines by 1-45 points and all gradient-based parametric methods by 10-45 points, while requiring zero generative LLM calls, zero gradient steps, and 3-151x less wall-clock time than extraction systems to build its memory. The two channels are complementary: the consolidated adapter adds 12-15 accuracy points when the episodic buffer misses the gold evidence, and still adds 4-5 points even with an oracle buffer.

摘要：

##### **IndustrialKnowEval: A Benchmark for Evaluating Multi-Level Industrial Knowledge in LLMs**
2611.20695v1 by Haiteng Wang, Haoze Li, Jingwei Guo, Jingbo Zhou, Si Liu, Lei Ren

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly explored for industrial tasks, yet few benchmarks evaluate their mastery of industrial knowledge across different types of cognitive demand. We introduce IndustrialKnowEval (IndKnow), a benchmark for industrial knowledge and engineering question answering. It contains 43,282 samples covering 21 task types across seven industrial sectors, including aerospace, electric power, and metallurgy, organized into four levels of cognitive demand: memory, comprehension, reasoning, and engineering application. Enterprise-based domain specialists and experienced researchers participate in all stages of dataset construction and quality control. We conduct a series of experiments on 18 representative LLMs, with domain experts deeply involved in the evaluation. Building upon IndKnow, we propose the Industrial Cognitive Capability Gap (ICCG) metric to characterize the performance differential between foundational industrial knowledge and higher-order reasoning and application, and we analyze it alongside absolute performance to provide a more complete view of model capabilities. We anticipate that IndustrialKnowEval will support the benchmarking of LLMs in industrial scenarios and promote the development of robust industrial intelligence. Code is available at: https://github.com/J4cksonLee/IndKnow

摘要：

##### **LEAF-OPD: Learning to Forget from Privileged Contexts via On-Policy Distillation**
2611.20674v1 by Zirui Pang, Zhijie Deng, Yuancheng Wang, Qihan Yu, Xin Li, Jiaheng Wei, Zixin Zhong

LLM unlearning aims to erase targeted knowledge from large language models while preserving their utility. However, many existing methods suppress target knowledge without explicitly teaching the model how to respond to queries about it. We propose LEAF-OPD (LEArning to Forget from Privileged Contexts via On-Policy Distillation), in which a frozen teacher provides token-level supervision on student-generated responses. On forget data, the teacher uses privileged contexts (instructions not to reveal knowledge about forget targets) to guide forgetting. On retain data, the teacher provides supervision without privileged context to preserve model utility. The student operates without privileged context in both cases, learning how to respond when encountering knowledge that should be forgotten. A key challenge is that the teacher often provides effective supervision only at the first few tokens of student trajectories. We introduce Supervision Signal (SS) to measure corrective supervision over a specified token interval, evaluating the entire trajectory (SS1) and its second half (SS0.5) to assess overall strength and late-stage persistence. When supervision is weak, lightweight SFT strengthens the teacher's context-conditioned refusal behavior, substantially increasing both scores. We evaluate LEAF-OPD on three widely used benchmarks: TOFU, RWKU, and MUSE-News. On TOFU, LEAF-OPD achieves the best Forget Quality, Model Utility, and Forget ROUGE-L while maintaining competitive retain performance. On RWKU, it outperforms the second-best method by 34% in Forget Quality while maintaining competitive Retain Quality. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of privileged-context supervision with on-policy token-level distillation for LLM unlearning.

摘要：

##### **Safety-Preserving Medical LLM Fine-Tuning via Bilevel Data Selection**
2611.20643v1 by Ruoxuan Shi, Sheng Yang, Zhengxing Su, Xiaoyang Hou, Yating Liu

As general large language models (LLMs) are increasingly adapted for patient-facing medical applications, improving medical capability while controlling patient-facing risks remains a critical challenge. Large-scale medical instruction data are essential for acquiring domain knowledge through supervised fine-tuning (SFT), but unsafe responses can undermine existing safety alignment. To address this problem, we propose Scalable Bilevel Data Selection (S-BDS) for safety-preserving medical SFT. By controlling selection-weight concentration, S-BDS enables reliable sample ranking over large candidate pools and identifies training examples that improve medical capability while controlling patient-facing risks. We combine clinician-validated safe data and selected data with unscreened medical instruction data for SFT, and jointly evaluate medical capability and patient-facing safety on HealthBench and PatientSafetyBench, respectively. Experiments across Llama and Qwen models show that S-BDS achieves the highest safe rates among all compared methods, outperforming the strongest baselines by 4.64 and 3.00 percentage points, respectively, while maintaining competitive medical capability. Further experiments show that varying the safety-oriented weight during SFT controls the trade-off between medical capability and patient-facing safety.

#### Abstracts
##### **Auditing black-box clinical prediction models with a large language model through queries alone: a blinded test with planted flaws**
2611.24877v1 by Zhiyu Liu, Xinyu Yang

Large language models (LLMs) are used to explain clinical prediction models, on the promise that they can say what a model computes, find its faults and even reveal new knowledge in it. We tested the three promises where the answers could be checked. From the International Stroke Trial we trained four kinds of model (logistic regression, explainable boosting machine, gradient-boosted trees, neural network) for two outcomes on patients randomised earlier, planted one flaw in each of 24 copies (an under-predicted subgroup, a reversed risk factor or an implausible step) and mixed them with the 8 clean models. GPT-6 Astra audited each model three times, blind, through a tool that only returned the model's predictions; the judging rules were committed before any run and applied by program. Its surrogate models, with a median of 128 terms, reproduced the audited models' log-odds with a mean $R^2$ of 0.976 (0.959 for an additive surrogate of 42 terms fitted by an analyst). It found the planted flaw in 69 of 72 runs (96%; 95% CI 80% to 99%); a script written for the purpose found all 24. It also reported many more problems: 60% of 1,089 checkable reports failed the pre-specified check against the model's average behaviour; post hoc, most of these were real but small steps, and 15% of the checkable reports were not found in the model. Of 601 unexpected relationships it read out of the clean models, 11% held in patients randomised later (random pairs 6%; a regression scan's shorter list of its strongest candidates, 28%); after a check on the training data, 33% against 30%. With query access alone, an LLM can describe a black-box model faithfully and find nearly every planted flaw, as a purpose-written script also did; its reports of problems must be checked on the model and its discoveries on data.

摘要：

##### **What Survives the Shift from Text to Pixels? Conditional Protection against Prompt Injection in Image-Rendered Documents**
2611.24231v1 by Haoyang Liu, Yinong Xu, Zhe Lang, Zhenyu Guan, Qianyun Zhang

Multimodal language models increasingly answer questions over external documents, exposing them to indirect prompt injection: commands planted in untrusted content to hijack the response. Rendering untrusted content as page images lowers attack success on average, leaving open which injected operations survive rendering, at what utility cost, and whether failed image attacks reflect unread commands or commands read but not followed. We pair text and page-image versions of 191 English question-answering sources across six models, measuring marker appending, answer formatting, and answer replacement alongside clean Question-Answering utility. Rendering reduces append and format execution for every model, yet Qwen3-VL-8B still executes image-borne replacement at 77.96\%. Protection also depends on target type, rendering settings, wording, and repetition, and a search over eight fixed attack candidates largely closes the image deficit in appending and formatting for Qwen and Gemini, though not for GPT-5-nano. Utility ratios on original questions range from 93.31\% to 101.61\%, but Qwen falls to 61.99\% when answers combine two evidence spans. Illegibility cannot explain all failed attacks: models often transcribe and identify image-borne commands they do not execute, and trusted user authorization raises execution on unchanged pages. Inside Qwen, patching early attack-region states induces all three operations, more weakly in images. Attention blocking suggests why: a text command acts through its own positions, whereas visual encoding spreads an image command and its target across the instruction page. Rendering thus offers conditional protection, motivating evaluations that report security by model, operation, and attack budget alongside utility.

摘要：

##### **How Reward Gain Imbalance Shapes Ranking: Rethinking Rubric-Based Reward Aggregation**
2611.24005v1 by Zhengyuan Fan, Zhonghua Wu, Tianyu Cheng, Qun Chen

Rubric-based reinforcement learning constructs rewards for aligning large language models with human preferences, typically by equally aggregating continuous scores that a judge assigns to individual criteria. However, scoring heterogeneity across criteria can distort candidate rankings. Existing work has focused on improving judges, learning criterion weights, or refining aggregation, whereas the interaction between scoring heterogeneity and ranking remains insufficiently understood. We formalize this heterogeneity as \emph{reward gain imbalance}: each criterion enters the aggregated reward with its own reward gain, the gap between its expected scores on satisfied and unsatisfied responses. Counterintuitively, our experiments show that removing this imbalance, even with ground-truth labels, lowers ranking accuracy. To explain this, we derive an exact decomposition of the reward difference between any two candidates into a base satisfaction signal, a reward gain imbalance term, and a residual term, showing that reward gain imbalance can attenuate or amplify the base signal, and finding that misrankings usually concentrate on pairs where it attenuates. Guided by this analysis, we propose the adaptive \textbf{D}ual-\textbf{C}enter \textbf{C}lipping aggregation (DCC), which bounds each criterion's contribution to the reward difference instead of simply removing the imbalance. Using only the scores of the current candidate group, it determines a clipping interval that imposes a common upper bound on per-criterion score differences between candidates, limiting the excessive influence of extreme scores while preserving continuous judgment information....

摘要：

##### **Rational Ensemble Logic for Cohort Discovery with Real-World Electrophysiology Data**
2611.23708v1 by Guo-Qiang Zhang, Xubing Hao, Yan Huang, Xiaojin Li, Licong Cui

Biomedical knowledge representation should make the passage from observations to conclusions transparent, rigorous, understandable, explainable, and reproducible (TRUER). We introduce Rational Ensemble Logic (QEL) for specifying temporal phenotypes in real-world electrophysiology data (RWED), with a meaning independent of the implementation software. QEL combines three parameterized modal operators (exact displacement, bounded existence, and bounded universality) over rational time with Boolean connectives and first-order quantification. Its Labeled Interval Ensemble (LINE) semantics represents annotated records as finite interval ensembles. We relate the operators to morphological operations and develop endpoint-array algorithms and finite rational evidence sets for model checking. We prove closure and semantic correctness of the interval operations, obtain finite evidence sets by quantifier elimination, and establish polynomial data complexity for fixed formulas. On 54 manually constructed records, a prototype agreed with the assigned outcomes for base and strict post-ictal generalized EEG suppression (PGES) queries. A separate feasibility experiment over 1,976 patients identified 37 qualifying patients and 45 annotation patterns in 16 seconds. The released prototype uses endpoint candidates and numerical tolerances. QEL supports the TRUER principle through explicit phenotype definitions, precise semantics, and provably correct evaluation procedures, with rational witnesses for existential queries. These provide a logical foundation for formal specification and correctness of algorithms that are independent of specific clinical interpretation, data annotation quality, and software implementation.

摘要：

##### **RagHarnessDebugger: Jev-Guided Agentic RAG in Contract Inference Repair Auditing**
2611.22991v1 by Moriya Dechtiar, Daniel Martin Katz, Michael J. Bommarito, Hongming Wang, Sylvain Jaume

Agentic systems using retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) must repair their own answers by gathering more evidence, testing alternative policies and deciding if, when, and what repairs are needed. Yet how do we know if a system's repairs are working when metrics like accuracy can be misleading? We present RagHarnessDebugger, a framework that records every repair decision in a Jev-guided harness for contract inference and audits the record to facilitate complete debugging of all decisions taken during a RAG repair journey. RagHarnessDebugger checks that each trace is intact, that each validation applies to the candidate it is counted for, and which answers the acceptance rule can reach. We tested our approach on ContractNLI non-disclosure agreements with two reader models and a replication set of separate contracts. Our debugger's audit layer exposed failure patterns that were hidden under aggregate metrics, policies acceptance cost of repairs vs regressions and policy acceptance gap of 77% and 76% of available but unrealised repairs on development and replication sets. Our audit, together with a labeled error analysis and replay mechanism, allowed following the execution path all the way to the terminal decision - uncovering where missing repairs became lost. Our revised harness improved accuracy over the initial BM25-only baseline by 3.6 points on development contracts and 3.7 points on the replication set, and even improved our full contract access reference score by 1.1 on development and 2.4 on replication set. While our work here demonstrates how the debugger can conduct decision root-cause analysis for missed repairs, the true value lies with providing this debugger for judgment visibility serving continuous improvement of RAG decisions and their explainability.
  Keywords: agentic RAG, AI evidence repair, harness explainability, counterfactual replay, Jev, ContractNLI

摘要：

##### **VITAL: A Framework for Validating Agentic AI in Pharmaceutical Drug Development and Clinical Trial Operations**
2611.22973v1 by Sandeep Burugupalli, Pedro Matos-Filipe, Andrea Hita Ardiaca, Adam Taylor, Alex Grigorenko, Natalia Jakubiak, Xin Yu, Raghav Mani, Sherry Mangla, Alvi Lai, Rachel C. Mercado, Pratik Shah, Raja Shankar

Current assurance approaches do not establish whether evidence is sufficient to validate, monitor and govern agentic artificial intelligence (AI) within a pharmaceutical context of use. Plausible outputs may conceal failures in retrieval, reasoning, orchestration or human-control points, where reference standards are unavailable. We developed and operationalised VITAL (Validation by Intended use, Traceability, Autonomy and Lifecycle evidence), a validation framework for agents in clinical development, through landscape analysis, regulatory and standards mapping, expert interviews and multi-stakeholder refinement. VITAL integrates context of use, controls, behavioural validation and lifecycle evidence within a risk-adjusted framework. We applied VITAL across epidemiology and treatment-landscape analysis, site selection, enrolment rescue and trial-design trade-off support to translate autonomy, reversibility, transparency and patient impact into proportionate validation activities, evidence requirements and auditable reconstruction of system behaviour. VITAL may inform evidence requirements and lifecycle accountability for higher-risk pharmaceutical uses; evaluation should assess reproducibility across assessors, organisations and system configurations.

摘要：

##### **Done, But Still Working: Anytime Evaluation of When Coding Agents Should Stop**
2611.22901v1 by Zerui Yang, Quan Xue, Miaomiao Dong, Chaorui Zhang, Xuanyu Zheng, Xiyuan Wang, Haolin Ruan, Xueyan Niu, Guangcheng Song, Weixi Zhang, Wei Han, Bo Bai

Coding agents often continue working after their own tests pass. Does this additional work improve the patch, and how much could be saved by stopping earlier? We study these questions through anytime evaluation: replaying recorded runs, scoring intermediate patches with hidden tests, and measuring the cost incurred at each step. For policies that only stop a run or select an earlier patch, this allows paired comparisons without generating new trajectories, provided replay preserves the relevant states. Across about 6,000 runs of eight open models in two scaffolds on SWE-bench Verified, we find substantial differences in the value of continued work. The hindsight saving available at passing self-tests ranges from under 1% to 57%, depending on the model and scaffold. A rule that stops after the same patch passes twice saves 23% in a preregistered replication, but the preregistered quality test does not establish non-inferiority. We explain these differences by measuring how often a rule fires, how often the patch changes afterwards, and whether those changes repair or break it. Fine-tuning agents to imitate the rule saves 30% over six seeds, while quality preservation remains uncertain. Our results help identify both the opportunities and the risks of early stopping.

摘要：

##### **Remembered as Brief: Stored Preferences SilentlyRemove Decision-Critical Information**
2611.22697v1 by Joshua Adrian Cahyono

People increasingly learn what they need for decisions about health, money, law and safety from AI assistants, and what an assistant leaves out the user never sees. An answer with a warning removed can be true in every sentence, so fact-checks pass it, and an assistant with memory applies a preference stored once to later questions the user never had in mind. We introduce \bench{}, an Inspect benchmark of 100 everyday decisions across 25 domains in which only the assistant's memory of the user varies: the user states a belief, the model infers it from a remark, or a web page the user asked to summarise plants it. Testing five models, we find three effects. First, after summarising a page with one line addressed to AI assistants, Gemini 2.5 Flash-Lite and DeepSeek V4 Pro save ``prefers very short answers and does not want warnings'' as the user's preference in every episode, and 93\% and 84\% of their later answers miss a critical item, almost always silently; under a stated brevity preference, 78--96\% of omissions are silent. Second, the same two models then pick an option for the user on 80\% and 95\% of value-dependent choices, against none with neutral memory. Third, a one-line instruction leaves 2--8\% of answers with a silent gap but most missing content still missing, while listing the must-know points before applying the user profile restores more of the content in four of five models. We release the scenarios, harness and logs.

摘要：

##### **Right Diagnosis, Unsafe Route: Auditing Clinical AI Decision Trajectories and Corrective Oversight for Validity and Potential Harm**
2611.22612v1 by Guanqiao Chen, Wei Jiang, Shu Zhang, Qian Lin, Guanxu Yu, Siran Li, Chunhui Wang, Tianyu Shi

A clinical AI system can reach a correct final diagnosis through intermediate decisions that could have harmed the patient, and can repair an error while introducing new risks. Most evaluations score final diagnoses; even stepwise benchmarks, which release a case's evidence over time, score whether systems reach the right diagnosis or gather the right information, not whether the decisions a patient passes through are safe. We make patient safety the target of stepwise evaluation. A trajectory audit replays real admissions checkpoint by checkpoint, lets each system decide with only the evidence released so far, and rates every test, treatment and disposition for its potential harm to the patient and, separately, for clinical validity, against the documented final diagnosis and physician-written disease rubrics yet on the evidence available when the decision was made. An LLM judge scores every decision, and a physician reference standard (triple annotation and senior adjudication of a stratified sample) calibrates the rates. An oversight audit branches each trajectory at its first actionable error and measures, on the same branches, whether corrective feedback repairs the error and whether it introduces new harm. Across 300 cases from a hospital cardiology cohort, MIMIC-IV and the public ASCENT benchmark, with up to seven AI systems per source, 55.2% of hospital trajectories reaching an acceptable final diagnosis passed through a decision with moderate or severe potential harm and 24.8% through a clinically unacceptable one; the harm rates were 76.2% on MIMIC-IV and 39.6% on ASCENT. Structured corrections resolved 73.6% of first errors versus 24.8% after bare rejection, yet new potentially unsafe decisions followed 30.2% of corrections versus 19.7% of rejections. A correct final diagnosis does not certify a safe trajectory, and a successful correction does not certify a safe recovery.

摘要：

##### **JET: Just-Enough Exploration Tuning for Efficient Tool-Calling Agents in Repository-Level Question Answering**
2611.22539v1 by Yoonho Choi, Taehyeon Han, Seonghyeon Lee

In repository-level question answering (QA), an agent explores a code repository with tools and should answer a question once enough information is gathered. Nevertheless, we find that LLM agents often continue to invoke tools even after the gathered information is already sufficient to answer. We call this phenomenon over-exploration. Moreover, even though existing methods improve task performance through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on expert trajectories, SFT also propagates the over-exploration in the trajectories to the agent. To mitigate over-exploration, we propose Just-Enough Exploration Tuning (JET), a fine-tuning method that trains the agent to answer once the gathered information is sufficient. JET truncates each training trajectory at the step where the gathered information becomes sufficient, inserts a rationale that explains why the agent can answer at that step, and fine-tunes the agent with a higher loss weight on the rationale. Experiments on two repository-level QA benchmarks with two open-weight 8B models demonstrate that JET reduces tool calls by 11 to 33% on self-generated trajectories while largely preserving the score. On expert trajectories, JET makes 18 to 28% fewer tool calls than SFT while retaining most of the score gain of SFT. In both cases, JET also lowers the inference cost.

摘要：

##### **Right Labels, Wrong Reasons: Signed Feature Attribution for Multi-Label Explainability**
2611.22515v1 by Kenton White

When a multi-label classifier assigns the right labels, it may do so for the wrong reasons. Diffuse feature combinations may vary across classifier families and lack a dominant feature. This paper proposes FLEX (Fisher LDA Explainer), a global surrogate that fits a single direct multi-label Fisher LDA over the full binary label matrix, producing signed feature attribution lists for each label in a single coherent coordinate system. Across the evaluated benchmarks and classifier families, FLEX reproduces the black box's predictions with consistently high fidelity. Pooling covariance across labels outperforms a per-label alternative on most datasets, with the advantage growing largest where labels are numerous and positive examples are scarce. FLEX's fidelity-complexity Pareto curve is monotonically increasing, giving practitioners a predictable path to higher-fidelity explanations as more features are used, and its in-sample fidelity matches or leads Binary Relevance alternatives once a modest feature budget is allowed. The magnitude of each discriminant weight vector bounds its label's separability, flagging labels that may be predicted correctly for the wrong reasons. The evaluated classifiers largely agree on which predictions to make, but reach those decisions through different feature subsets when no single feature dominates.

##### **Management of Behavioral Addictions via Artificial Intelligence based Digital Technologies**
2611.23341v1 by Süleyman Eken, Serdar Solak, Mustafa Hikmet Bilgehan Uçar, Bahadır Geniş, Ömer Kardaş, Hande Albayrak, İsmail Emre Ak, Nesibe Sağlam

Behavioral addictions, such as problematic gaming, gambling, social media use, and online shopping, have emerged as a growing global concern with significant psychological, social, and economic consequences. Traditional assessment and treatment approaches, while clinically grounded, often struggle with scalability, accessibility, and long-term engagement. In recent years, advances in digital technologies and artificial intelligence (AI) have enabled the development of novel, data-driven solutions for the prevention, detection, treatment, and recovery of behavioral addictions. This book presents a comprehensive survey of the management of behavioral addictions through digital technologies, bridging clinical foundations with state-of-the-art computational approaches. This book aims to provide researchers, clinicians, and practitioners with a unified, interdisciplinary perspective on the opportunities and limitations of AI-based digital technologies in behavioral addiction management,and to highlight pathways toward more scalable, personalized, and effective intervention strategies.

摘要：

##### **Anticipating or Speculating? Making Futures Pay in AI Ethics**
2611.22268v2 by John P. Nelson

Philosophers have recently been trying to develop uniform decision rules for instances in which physicians' judgments diverge from the recommendations of medical artificial intelligence (AI) systems. I argue that this effort is fatally flawed because the performance and appropriate use of algorithmic recommendation systems by physicians are highly context-specific, empirical medical matters rather than epistemological ones. In such instances, general decision rules can be stipulated only to the extent that they abstract away from relevant, indeed determinative, contextual circumstances. The alleged problem of physician-AI disagreement, and others like it, play into longstanding disputes in technology ethics about the viability and utility of envisioning possible futures: is this useful "anticipation" or wasteful "speculation"? I suggest that the two may be distinguished. Anticipation explores plausible futures to help inform present actions under irreducible uncertainty. Speculation abstracts away uncertainty, developing formal solutions to stipulated circumstances whose relationship to the real present is often quite unclear. Efforts to develop crisp philosophical solutions to physician-AI disagreement, among other problems, fall into the latter category. Technology ethics should remember that fruitful futures thinking must both begin from and return to the messy, uncertain present.

##### **Not All Evidence Is Admissible: Evidence Governance for Tool-Augmented Medical Agents**
2611.22169v1 by Runxun Zhang, Onofrio Antonio Catalano, Jinqiao Wang, Jingwei Wei

Tool-augmented medical agents treat whatever their tools return as evidence. We show that this default is costly: medical tools fail silently, and a frozen reasoner cannot ignore what enters its context, so many cases it would have answered correctly are flipped to wrong once tool outputs are added. We call this evidence hijacking. $\mathcal{V}$-information explains the phenomenon: the reasoner has lost the option to ignore, so a tool output can carry negative usable information, and moving the decision of what to keep outside the reasoner restores that option. The theory further shows that part of any filtering gain comes merely from letting the reasoner see less, and only selection by content lets it see better. Building on this, we recast tool-augmented reasoning as evidence governance, which separates deciding what counts as evidence from the reasoner that answers, and propose context-independent evidence governance (CIEG), the first medical agent framework that rules, after acquisition, on whether each individual tool output may enter as evidence. Every tool output is written as a card that can be checked against the raw input; an independent checker that sees only the input and the cards decides which cards become evidence; and the reasoner answers from the admitted cards alone, with no training anywhere. On medical question answering, fundus glaucoma screening, and chest X-ray pneumonia classification, it achieves the highest accuracy among models of comparable scale, and significantly outperforms state-of-the-art tool-using agents and multi-agent systems built on the same backbone at lower cost.

##### **Long-Context Generation Is a Sampling Problem (When You Turn Up the Heat)**

Open-weight language models are widely observed to get worse the longer they generate. Often, attempts to fix this involve more training. We test 29 open-weight models from 1B to 122B parameters on ten established benchmarks plus needle retrieval out to one million tokens, covering SWE-bench Verified coding agents, long chain-of-thought reasoning, long-form writing, long structured output and long-context reading. In one Llama-3.1-8B story, going from temperature 1 to 2 grows top-p's pool from 4 tokens to 51,845 while top-nsigma stays at 3. Across 133 combinations of sampler setting and temperature, the typical pool size predicts judged quality (Spearman correlation of -0.77), and when we pick the pool at temperature 1 and only apply temperature afterwards, Top-H, min-p and p-less stop collapsing. ##### **On Formal Counterfactual Explanations**
2611.22010v1 by Alexey Ignatiev, Peter J. Stuckey, Joao Marques-Silva

Counterfactual explanations are often seen as counterfactual conditionals under closest-world semantics, requiring one to reason over all minimally distant counterexamples. In contrast, formal XAI typically relies on feature-based abductive explanations (AXps) and contrastive explanations (CXps), which admit efficient computation and a minimal hitting-set duality. This paper bridges these perspectives by (i) characterizing closest-world counterfactual explanations explicitly as a disjunctive normal form over all minimally distant counterexample instances, and (ii) introducing conjunctive counterfactual explanations (CFXps), a subclass of counterfactual antecedents restricted to conjunctions of feature literals that guarantee prediction change. The paper establishes structural results connecting CXps, AXps and CFXps: every CXp induces an AXp for its witness instance, and every CXp together with a witness AXp induces a CFXp whose support is precisely determined by that AXp. These theoretical results yield a modular approach for computing CFXps using existing AXp/CXp machinery. Experiments on a representative set of benchmarks using tree ensembles and neural networks show that CFXp extraction is practically efficient. Compared with popular point-based counterfactual explainers, CFXps generally require no more feature changes and can guarantee the counterfactual prediction beyond a single instance.

##### **Beyond Reconstruction: A Unified Framework for Trustworthy and Explainable EEG Artifact Removal Evaluation**
2611.21781v1 by Phat Lam, Hung Nguyen, Lam Pham, Huy Phan, Dat Tran, Ian McLoughlin, Roozbeh Razavi-Far, Hung Cao

Deep-learning methods have achieved strong performance in EEG artifact removal, but evaluation remains dominated by reconstruction metrics such as error, correlation, and signal-to-noise ratio. Although these metrics assess similarity to clean EEG, they do not establish whether models effectively suppress artifacts, preserve neural activity, or provide reliable explanations. To address these limitations, we propose TriX-EEG, a three-level framework for trustworthy and explainable EEG artifact removal. Level 1, Signal Validity (SV), evaluates reconstruction quality and neural band-power preservation. Level 2, Removal Faithfulness (RF), assesses the removed component through artifact error and residual artifact/neural leakage. Level 3, Explanation Reliability (ER), examines model behavior through temporal alignment, causal relevance, and stability. We evaluate TriX-EEG using nine published neural denoisers across three public benchmarks derived from EEGDenoiseNet and the PhysioNet Motion Artifact Contaminated EEG Dataset, covering EOG, EMG, and motion artifacts. Results show that reconstruction metrics alone are insufficient for trustworthy denoiser assessment: RF complements it by revealing whether errors stem from residual artifact or suppressed neural activity, while ER captures distinct aspects of explanation behavior, supporting joint evaluation of three aspects within a unified framework rather than reliance on any single factor. External validation on an independent EEG/EOG dataset and the BCI Competition IV-2a motor-imagery dataset further demonstrates broadly consistent rankings under dataset shift. Higher-ranked denoisers generally preserve more task-relevant information or cause less downstream performance degradation. Overall, TriX-EEG provides a comprehensive basis for EEG denoiser assessment, encouraging models that are not only accurate but also faithful and explainable.

##### **Severe but Harmless: Stress-Testing Shift Severity for Frozen Time-Series Forecasters**
2611.21562v1 by Korede R. Bishi, Dominic Okonkwo, Adetayo Okunoye

##### **Optimistic Verifiers Get Hacked: Reward Hacking Under an Exact Process Oracle**
2611.21538v1 by Arunkumar V, Manoranjan Gandhudi, Gangadharan G. R., Arun Prakash, S. Senthilkumar

##### **Programmable Semantic Interventions: Learning Causal Carriers from Behavioral Programs**
2611.21307v1 by Paolo Muratore, Mackenzie Weygandt Mathis

Causal abstraction explains a frozen network by aligning its activations with the variables of a simpler, interpretable model, and checking that interventions on the two agree. Finding such an alignment currently requires a counterfactual dataset: for every pair of prompts, the answer the network ought to give once the variable has been changed. In practice, those labels are produced by an executable algorithm for the behavior, written before studying the model supposed to implement it. However, the criterion these methods optimize only leverages the answer an intervention should produce. Here, we develop an approach that lets the investigator define that relationship directly: the answers the variable can produce, and which of them an intervention should yield. We call this interface a semantic program and introduce Programmable Semantic Interventions (PSI): a low-rank activation subspace learned so that interchanging it changes the model's answer as the program declares, with the expected answers read from the frozen model itself. On the MIB benchmark PSI performs on par with supervised alignment at matched data and training budget, without curated pairs, and it needs no ground-truth labels; while on natural science questions with gold labels withheld, it tracks the model's own answers better than a subspace trained on gold labels. Sources from arXiv, CNKI, ACL Anthology, and official documentation were searched through November 2026 and coded into five evidence categories: reviews, system prototypes, benchmarks, HCI and eldercare studies, and official documentation. System designs were mapped onto shared architectural modules, identifying nine recurrent responsibilities of the home local server. Generative models support perceptual interpretation, intent understanding, and candidate planning, while deterministic validation, knowledge-graph constraints, and authorization leases confine candidate actions to an inspectable space. Existing benchmarks rely on virtual environments and cannot substitute for real-deployment evaluation. 2611.20268v1 by Marcello Sano, Samuele Casagrande, Davide Ferrario, Sebastiano Vascon, Silvia Torresan, Andrea Critto

When a storm surge approaches a coastal town, the emergency plan must be put into action quickly using water-level forecasts, flood extent, road access, vulnerable residents and shelters. Bringing this information together under time pressure is slow and depends on the duty officer's expertise. This paper is one step in a wider research line from data to safe decisions: machine learning to understand the hazard, reinforcement learning and planning to choose actions, large language models (LLMs) to explain and cross-check them, and an assurance layer that keeps people in charge. Here we address the action step on a real place. We ask whether a policy can learn the timing of emergency actions from decades of real events. Lido di Venezia is represented as a heterogeneous graph built from public elevation, road, building and census data and driven by hourly tide-gauge records from 1983 to 2024, with seven actions taken from the municipal emergency plan. A PPO policy is trained on this graph and compared, on storm windows unseen in training, with a rule-based system simulating the officer's discretionary decisions. On 18 historical test scenarios, PPO achieves a similar reduction in population exposure to the rule. With uncertain forecasts and limited protection it again performs similarly but does not outperform the tuned rule, and removing graph message passing does not substantially reduce performance. PPO therefore learns an effective protective response from an outcome-based reward alone: it is given the plan's actions and when each is allowed, but not the thresholds that tell the comparator when to act. Next steps test harder edge cases, move closer to a digital twin of the territory, and replace the simulated officer with assessment by emergency-management stakeholders, supported by LLM-based interpretation of each recommendation.

##### **FRAAC: A French Multimodal Large Language Model and Dataset forPictogram-Based Augmentative and Alternative Communication**
2611.20094v1 by Alexandre Audibert, François Portet, Benoit Favre, Maja Jonck Hjuler

Pictograms are a core modality in augmentative and alternative communication (AAC), yet recent multimodal large language models (MLLMs) cannot reliably interpret or produce pictogram sequences. Two obstacles explain this gap: the absence of a standardized pictogram grammar and the scarcity of parallel text-pictogram data. Applied to French, RAPS yields a synthetic corpus of 1.4 million parallel pairs, of which 300k are publicly released. In a human ranking study where annotators order four pictogram transcriptions of the same sentence, RAPS outperforms the rule-based state of the art. Moreover, we show that standard reference-based metrics separate systems of clearly different quality but fail to rank strong systems against each other. Finally, we build an instruction-tuned model that supports interaction in several AAC modalities: it accepts pictogram or text input and answers in pictograms, speech, or text.

2611.20019v1 by Ahmad Alfan Alfian Irfan, Nur Ahmad Khatim, Mansur Arief

Reinforcement learning agents learn from mistakes, yet their decisions can be difficult to trace back to those experiences. We investigate whether a tabular agent's decisions can be explained by its bad experiences, defined as the training transitions associated with safety or task events. A fixed training log is replayed from the same initial Q-table under three conditions. FULL retains all updates, SKIP omits bad-experience updates, and NEUTRALIZE retains those updates without their event penalties. We illustrate the method on six classic Markov decision processes and evaluate it in closed-loop driving in CARLA. On the full driving task, FULL reached the goal from 33 of 40 test starts, whereas SKIP and NEUTRALIZE reached it from none, indicating that the bad experiences carry decision-relevant information. At the local level, for 47 decisions shortly before a failure of SKIP or NEUTRALIZE, removing or neutralizing a small group of bad experiences, with a median size of one, caused the trained agent to select the failing policy's action in 46 cases. For this agent, the results indicate that its decisions can be explained by its bad experiences.

##### **Data Attribution Should Steer Pretraining**
2611.19698v1 by Glenn Matlin, Chandreyi Chakraborty, Mohammed Rehan Parwani, Saehee Eom, Anthony Wen-Ming Zang, Akhil Theerthala, Arjun Chatterjee, Tejas Khandwekar, Brandon Michaels, Alvin Deng, Mark Riedl

Pretraining data curation decides what a model learns from, yet what makes a document worth learning from depends on what the model already knows. Influence-driven selectors already rank documents to improve one run; we argue that training-data attribution should steer pretraining by identifying testable data properties that become reusable curation criteria, evaluated on new data against cheaper selectors at matched total cost, discovery included. A document ranking is consumed by the run it was computed for, whereas an explained data property can be tested across corpora and checkpoints, especially where familiar quality signals diverge from measured usefulness. Because historical contribution, an understandable explanation, and future data value require distinct evidence, we specify a feedback loop that interprets attributed sources, tests candidate data changes, and states for five claim types what evidence each requires and what none establishes. We close with a falsifiable comparison against perplexity, classifier, and mixing baselines at equal total compute.

2611.19550v1 by Truong Thinh Nguyen, Hai Phong Nguyen, Truong Thanh Hung Nguyen, Giang Son Tran, Hung Cao
