Neither captures what clinicians actually complain about. Recent work has shown that evaluation criteria can be mined from user feedback and filtered to criteria that LLM judges can reliably enforce. We argue that this is where much of the value is lost. The criteria that fail enforceability are disproportionately the ones clinicians raise most often, because high-frequency complaints are stated as several questions glued together. No yes/no judgment can grade such composites as posed. We introduce an objectification step that rewrites such criteria into sets of scoped, determinate decisions. We demonstrate the approach through an evaluation pipeline that mines criteria from complaints, objectifies them, and deploys LLM judges against both planted defects and production notes. Across eleven defects planted into ACI-bench reference notes, objectified judges localize violations with median recall of 0.95, while reference metrics change by at most 0.037. Given the complaint as one broad question, the same judges still find every planted defect, but they also flag 70% of the unperturbed reference notes; one section-pair question per call raises Youden's J from 0.30 to 0.72 (by 0.19 to 0.26 with prompt effort matched). On 1,446 production notes, mean instance-rate burden falls at every clinician-rating step (rho=-0.15), a small association. Defect counts have to be kept per span rather than collapsed into one pass/fail bit per note.

##### **LABEL: Literature Asymmetry between Efficacy and Limits in Drug Knowledge and Language Models**
2611.19166v1 by S M Rayeed, Charles V. Stewart, Yao Ma

Every medication comes with two types of medical facts: indications, which describe what conditions the drug treats (benefits), and contraindications, which describe who must avoid it to prevent serious harm (safety limits). Although contraindications are critical for patient safety and FDA regulations require listing them as rigorously as indications, we find that contraindications are far less documented than indications in general prose. In this paper, we measure how often these texts contain both facts across 574 drugs from PrimeKG by comparing a drug's indications against its own contraindications. Across Wikipedia articles and PubMed abstracts, we find that reference prose names a drug's benefits about twice as often as its safety limits (within-drug odds ratio 2.19). We call this documentation gap LABEL (Literature Asymmetry between Efficacy and Limits). Language models mirror this gap. As we scale Qwen2.5 from 0.5B to 14B, accuracy on indications rises by 18.3 points, whereas accuracy on contraindications remains flat near random guessing. However, when we provide models with a retrieved passage that states the answer, they use safety facts just as effectively as benefits. Furthermore, routing safety questions to official FDA labels boosts accuracy by 15.8 points whenever the label states the fact. Overall, our findings show that models fail on medication safety not because they cannot reason about risk, but because free-text corpora lack safety facts. Clinical AI systems should therefore route medication-safety questions to official regulatory labels and structured databases rather than relying on general public records. Code and data are available at https://github.com/SMRayeed/LABEL.

##### **From Sensors to Silicon: Causal Cross-Layer Self-Healing Edge AI for Secure and Energy-Adaptive IoT Systems**
2611.19165v1 by Mostafa Darvishi

The migration of artificial intelligence from centralized cloud infrastructure to resource-constrained Internet-of-Things (IoT) devices reduces communication latency and improves data locality, but it also tightly couples sensing quality, inference behavior, microarchitectural activity, hardware reliability, security, and energy management within the same physical node. Existing edge-intelligence solutions predominantly address these dimensions in isolation, making it difficult to determine whether an observed degradation originates from sensor malfunction, data-distribution shift, malicious execution, hardware faults, or resource and thermal stress. This paper presents a causal cross-layer self-healing Edge-AI framework that continuously observes synchronized telemetry spanning sensor health, inference uncertainty, processor hardware-performance counters, accelerator and memory activity, power and thermal state, and communication conditions. A temporally informed causal reasoning engine estimates the most probable origin of abnormal behavior and quantifies diagnostic uncertainty, including under concurrent perturbations.

##### **Effects of Conventional, Adaptive, and AI-Powered Upscaling on the Legibility of License Plates**
2611.18949v1 by Hany Farid, Joshua D. Bucci, Emily A. Cooper

Small details in digital images -- a face, a street sign, a license plate -- are routinely upscaled in hopes of making them interpretable. Upscaling options now range from conventional interpolation, whose operations are spatially invariant and fully explainable, to AI-powered methods that synthesize detail potentially absent from the source. Here, we ask which of these techniques most reliably helps human readers. In two online experiments, we measured legibility of 255 US license plates ranging in resolution from 128 to 8 pixels tall, as well as their legibility after upscaling by eight different methods. Legibility of the un-upscaled plates collapsed from ceiling to floor across a narrow band of plate heights (32 to 10 pixels). Conventional upscaling techniques (bilinear, bicubic, and Lanczos interpolation) gave modest gains of about 5-9 percentage points within this range. Nearest-neighbor interpolation, however, was harmful, rendering unreadable many plates that were readable without any upscaling. Advanced methods diverged: SwinIR, an open-source AI enhancer, raised performance by up to 38 points relative to no upscaling, while an adaptive denoiser-based image prior and commercial AI upscaler tended to impair legibility. We found no strong evidence of consistent character hallucinations across methods. Decomposing the task, conventional interpolators mainly helped readers count characters, whereas SwinIR alone made the characters themselves more identifiable.

In this work, we study Continual Recursive Self-Improvement (CRSI), a setting for continuing existing harness-optimization methods across compatible benchmarks or policy models. We propose Self-Improvement Memory (SIM) for the harness optimizer, rather than the task-solving policy. SIM groups edits by recurring harness problem and records their rationale, predicted and observed outcomes, and evaluation context. New attempts extend this history as optimization continues. 2611.18514v1 by Mohamed Aghzal, Mor Geva, Gregory J. Stein, Ziyu Yao

Mechanistic Interpretability (MI) has made significant progress in explaining the internal computations of Large Language Models (LLMs); however, these advances have yet to demonstrate comparable success in explaining the decision-making processes of LLM-based agents. This paper examines the challenges MI tools face when applied to realistic agentic failures. We focus on one such failure: web agents hallucinating actions that are not available on a website. We apply a series of mechanistic analyzes to Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct as it interacts with live websites. We find that a linear probe trained on the last prompt token can predict hallucinations before generation begins (0.80 AUC) and steering along a probe direction corrects 85\% of hallucinations, but disrupts 7\% of valid actions. The probe direction is sensitive to prompt formatting, losing much of its steering effect after a minor change to how actions are represented. AI-enabled remote patient monitoring is evolving beyond passive data collection toward agentic AI systems that interpret patient states, coordinate care, and initiate action across domestic and clinical settings. ##### **Dancing Tokens on the Vocabulary Network from LLM Generation**
2611.18248v1 by Yuan-Chao Hu

Large language models (LLMs) generate text one token at a time, yet how a particular generation relates to the geometry learned in pre-training remains unclear. The fundamental explainability of LLMs is elusive. Here, we build a similarity network from the input embeddings of six open LLMs and map generation onto it. The token-embedding network is scale-free while heavy-tailed, as are random embeddings with matched covariance, but far more fragmented. Distillation barely changes this topology because input embeddings are largely inherited. The knowledge space is thus deterministic from pre-training. Consecutive generated tokens are rarely graph neighbours and, in most models, no more often than in shuffled order, so generation is not a walk on the predefined network, although the tokens it visits are concentrated within it. Through the logit lens in a layer- wise manner, tokens settle late in depth in most models, and repetitive models remain confined to small domain-specific sub-graphs. These observables could serve as inference-time monitors of degeneration. We thus provide a fresh perspective on understanding LLM generation in the microscopic way from node or link prediction by graph learning.

##### **The Transformer Paradigm: Dynamic Processing and a New Principle of Inference**
2611.17953v1 by Marco Giunti, Fabrizia Giulia Garavaglia

We argue that the Transformer revolution is not adequately understood as a merely quantitative expansion of deep learning, but as the emergence of a new paradigm based on a different principle of inference. We interpret the Transformer as a system in which token representations are transformed by parameterized transformations, or transforming concepts. During inference, some of these transformations are dynamically constructed from the current input: previously acquired transformations are first adapted and then applied. At the mechanical-structural level, this dynamic processing is realized through output-weight interconnections, whereby outputs of some networks determine the weights of others. The resulting mode of processing, which we call Sequence-level Interactive Dynamic Parallel Processing (SIDPP), provides a framework for explaining strong prompt sensitivity and opens research directions concerning interpretability, control, alternative AI architectures, neuromorphic systems, and possible biological realizations of dynamic processing.

As Artificial Intelligence (AI)-based systems have become increasingly integrated into various aspects of everyday life, including education, understanding how these systems make decisions is of paramount importance. However, the opacity of AI-based decision-making in education (especially with deep learning and generative AI models) can hamper decision-making, especially when outcomes appear unjustified or incorrect. In this chapter, we argue that three key principles (uncertainty, explainability, and human responsibility) are essential for the legitimate use of AI in educational settings. Acknowledging the inherent uncertainties in AI-based decisions, analogous to those in human decision-making, is fundamental. Explainability is critical for ensuring that the functioning and outcomes of AI applications are transparent and comprehensible to both educators and learners. Furthermore, human responsibility is crucial, particularly in human decision-making, which remains indispensable across all stages of AI development and deployment. By examining these three principles, this chapter provides a comprehensive guide for stakeholders seeking to integrate AI thoughtfully and effectively into the educational system.

##### **SPADE: Soft Prompts for Ai-text DEtection**

The easy access to Large Language Models (LLMs) has made AI-generated texts on the web increasingly ubiquitous, making AI-text detection a crucial task. Prompts greatly influence the generation process, but existing detectors focus solely on the generated texts --- supervised methods learn from labeled texts only, and zero-shot methods rely on the text's probability distributions --- ignoring the underlying generation process, which limits their robustness in open-world scenarios. We introduce SPADE: Soft Prompts for Ai-text DEtection, a novel framework for detecting AI-generated text that transfers better across datasets. Specifically, we measure the prompt's influence and use it as a discriminative signal. We characterize the prompt's influence by introducing a Pointwise Mutual Information (PMI) term that captures how much a prompt explains a text. Based on this quantity, SPADE trains one soft prompt per class to amplify the separation, and predicts the class whose prompt best explains the candidate text. Extensive experiments on several benchmarks against various detectors demonstrate the robustness and effectiveness of our method, achieving state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance on out-of-distribution (OOD) datasets.

##### **From Data to Wisdom: Rethinking AI Through an Epistemic Lens**
2611.16726v2 by Afonso Eduardo

This paper proposes the Data-to-Wisdom Epistemic Architecture for AI (EAAI), a conceptual framework treating Data, Information, Knowledge, Understanding and Wisdom as five distinct but recursively interacting epistemic functions: observation, contextual interpretation, modeling and prediction, critical evaluation and informed judgment. Two observations motivate it: sequential readings of the Knowledge Pyramid obscure how these functions interact, and a targeted review of twelve Bayesian reference books finds that prior distributions are defined mathematically while "prior information" and "prior knowledge" are not consistently distinguished as epistemic categories. A Bayesian interpretation relates the likelihood, prior and posterior distributions, posterior prediction, model checking and decision analysis to the five functions, without limiting EAAI to Bayesian systems. Extending "prior" in an epistemic sense, the paper distinguishes five kinds of prior context that can shape the model, the likelihood, the prior, evaluation and action. Three worked applications -- quantitative financial risk, actuarial modeling in health insurance and agentic AI for medical reimbursement claims -- show how EAAI structures the analysis of model assumptions, evidence interpretation, validation, dependencies and decision consequences, separating the prediction of a risk, the appraisal of its reliability and the justification of action. It draws implications for AI and machine learning, including evaluation by function, treating intermediate model outputs as evidence open to challenge and separating generation from appraisal, and compares EAAI with work on DIKW, Bayesian workflow, AI evaluation and risk governance. Its contributions are an epistemic vocabulary with recursive relationships, a more accessible reading of Bayesian methodology, a typology of prior context and clearer methodological terminology

The uniqueness claim is conditional; for $k<d_z-1$, where $d_z$ is the number of world states, this limit makes its premise unsatisfiable, so the claim has no content there. This view explains existing methods, organizes them into a common framework, and suggests new algorithms by replacing one component while keeping the others, with composition of energies as a principled way to combine learned models at inference time.

  METHODS: LUCQUE was curated and is hosted on PARADIM, a digital infrastructure supporting DICOM data de-identification, annotation, long-term storage and analysis. LDCT scans and clinical information were combined to a curated collection and complemented by voxel-level, expert-derived pulmonary nodule and cyst segmentations, and Lung-RADS-based lesion characteristics. Key dataset characteristics and statistics were reported, including participant demographics, imaging parameters, radiological findings, and segmented lesion characteristics. Dataset analyses were conducted according to PARADIM's model-to-data approach, in which user code is executed on the platform such that data remains on premise.
  RESULTS: LUCQUE currently comprises 1,678 LDCT baseline studies from 1,678 participants. Most studies were classified as Lung-RADS 2. A total of 1,776 pulmonary nodules and 60 cysts were annotated, with a median (interquartile range) volume of $89\,(56,\,159)\,\mathrm{mm}^3$ and $1,384\,(589,\,3793)\,\mathrm{mm}^3$ respectively.
  CONCLUSIONS: Compared to publicly available LCS datasets, LUCQUE currently represents the largest accessible annotated real-world LCS dataset. Access to LUCQUE is provided via a model-to-data approach, enabling privacy-preserving, reproducible, and independent real-world evaluation of AI tools in LCS.

摘要：

##### **LogCDM: Interpretable Log Anomaly Detection via Behavioral Graph Drift and Adaptive Signal Fusion**
2611.12775v1 by Suryansh Talukdar, Taha Sarwat, Jin Soung Yoo

Log-based anomaly detection is increasingly dominated by large pretrained models, from BERT-scale encoders to 8B-parameter language models, which require GPUs at inference time and are difficult to deploy on the monitoring nodes where logs are produced. We present LogCDM, a compact detector that scores each log session using three complementary signals: the next-event surprise of a small causal Transformer, the distance between a graph-attention embedding of the session's own transition graph and that of a normal reference graph, and a transition-drift statistic that we show is exactly a frequency-weighted chi-squared divergence between transition kernels. The divergence view yields an asymptotic null distribution, a length-standardized score, and a per-edge decomposition that explains each alarm. A sigmoid gate fuses the three signals; under a shared-covariance Gaussian model, this gate is the Bayes posterior, and its learned weights indicate which signal each dataset relies on. With 8.1-8.8M parameters and no GPU required at inference, LogCDM reaches F1 scores of 0.984 on HDFS, 0.870 on chronologically split BGL, and 0.850 on Thunderbird, within 0.013-0.12 of an 8.1B-parameter LLM detector evaluated under the same protocol and above all compact baselines on BGL. It scores a session in 5.5-10.5 ms on a CPU. Ablations show that the structural signals add 0.025-0.030 F1 on BGL and Thunderbird but almost nothing on HDFS, consistent with the learned fusion weights.
